### Q1. MHT CET 2026 (20 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

What is degree of dissociation of $\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4$ if the observed elevation in boiling point of electrolytic solution is $0.1352^\circ$C and normal elevation in boiling point of a non-electrolytic solution of same concentration is $0.052^\circ$C ?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.5$
- **(B)** $0.6$
- **(C)** $0.7$
- **(D)** $0.8$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $0.8$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The van't Hoff factor $i$ is the ratio of the observed elevation in boiling point to the normal elevation in boiling point.

$i = \dfrac{\Delta T_{b(\text{obs})}}{\Delta T_{b(\text{normal})}} = \dfrac{0.1352}{0.052} = 2.6$

The dissociation of $\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4$ is given by:

$\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4 \rightarrow 2\text{K}^+ + \text{SO}_4^{2-}$

The number of ions produced per formula unit is $n = 3$.

The relationship between the van't Hoff factor $i$ and the degree of dissociation $\alpha$ is:

$i = 1 + (n - 1)\alpha$

$2.6 = 1 + (3 - 1)\alpha$

$2.6 = 1 + 2\alpha$

$2\alpha = 1.6$

$\alpha = 0.8$

Answer: $0.8$

</details>

### Q2. MHT CET 2026 (20 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which of the following solutions is isotonic to $0.1$ M NaCl solution at the same temperature, assuming complete dissociation of NaCl ? (Molar mass of glucose = $180$ g/mol)

**Options:**

- **(A)** A solution containing $18$ g glucose in $500$ ml solution.
- **(B)** A solution containing $1.8$ g glucose in $1000$ ml solution.
- **(C)** A solution containing $3.6$ g glucose in $500$ ml solution.
- **(D)** A solution containing $7.2$ g glucose in $250$ ml solution.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) A solution containing $18$ g glucose in $500$ ml solution.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Osmotic pressure is given by $\pi = iCRT$.

For $0.1$ M NaCl solution, assuming complete dissociation, $i = 2$.

$\pi_{NaCl} = 2 \times 0.1 \times RT = 0.2 RT$

For an isotonic glucose solution, the osmotic pressure must be equal to $0.2 RT$. Since glucose does not dissociate, $i = 1$.

$\pi_{glucose} = 1 \times C \times RT = 0.2 RT$

$C = 0.2$ M

Checking the given options for the concentration of glucose:

For the solution containing $18$ g glucose in $500$ ml solution:

Moles of glucose = $\dfrac{18}{180} = 0.1$ mol

Volume of solution = $500$ ml = $0.5$ L

Molarity $C = \dfrac{0.1}{0.5} = 0.2$ M

This matches the required concentration.

Answer: A solution containing $18$ g glucose in $500$ ml solution.

</details>

### Q3. MHT CET 2026 (19 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

One litre of glucose solution have osmotic pressure $1.5$ atmosphere is mixed with other $2$ litre glucose solution of osmotic pressure $2.5$ atmosphere. Then find resultant osmotic pressure of the solution.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.217$ atm
- **(B)** $2.17$ atm
- **(C)** $21.0$ atm
- **(D)** $216$ atm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $2.17$ atm**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the formula for the resultant osmotic pressure of a mixture of solutions of the same solute:

$\pi_{mix} = \dfrac{\pi_1 V_1 + \pi_2 V_2}{V_1 + V_2}$

Substituting the given values:

$\pi_1 = 1.5$ atm, $V_1 = 1$ L

$\pi_2 = 2.5$ atm, $V_2 = 2$ L

$\pi_{mix} = \dfrac{1.5 \times 1 + 2.5 \times 2}{1 + 2}$

$\pi_{mix} = \dfrac{1.5 + 5.0}{3}$

$\pi_{mix} = \dfrac{6.5}{3} = 2.166... \approx 2.17$ atm

Answer: $2.17$ atm

</details>

### Q4. MHT CET 2026 (19 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Addition of $0.643$ g of compound to $50$ ml of benzene (density of benzene $= 0.879$ g/ml) lowers the freezing point from $5.51^\circ\text{C}$ to $5.03^\circ\text{C}$. If $K_f$ for benzene is $5.12$ K kg/mol. Calculate the molecular weight of a compound in gram?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $165$
- **(B)** $156$
- **(C)** $137$
- **(D)** $173$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $156$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Mass of benzene, $W = \text{Volume} \times \text{Density} = 50 \times 0.879 = 43.95$ g

Depression in freezing point, $\Delta T_f = 5.51 - 5.03 = 0.48^\circ\text{C}$

Using the formula for depression in freezing point:

$\Delta T_f = \dfrac{1000 \times K_f \times w}{M \times W}$

Substituting the given values:

$0.48 = \dfrac{1000 \times 5.12 \times 0.643}{M \times 43.95}$

$M = \dfrac{3292.16}{21.096} \approx 156$ g/mol

Answer: $156$

</details>

### Q5. MHT CET 2026 (19 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A solution containing $0.6 \text{ g}$ nonelectrolyte solute in $30 \text{ g}$ water freezes at $-0.6\ ^\circ\text{C}$. Calculate the molar mass of solute if cryoscopic constant of water is $1.8 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $54 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$.
- **(B)** $60 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$.
- **(C)** $66 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$.
- **(D)** $72 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $60 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given:

Mass of solute, $w_2 = 0.6 \text{ g}$

Mass of solvent, $w_1 = 30 \text{ g}$

Depression in freezing point, $\Delta T_f = 0 - (-0.6) = 0.6 \text{ K}$

Cryoscopic constant, $K_f = 1.8 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$

The formula for depression in freezing point is:

$\Delta T_f = K_f \times m$

where $m$ is the molality of the solution.

$\Delta T_f = \dfrac{K_f \times w_2 \times 1000}{M_2 \times w_1}$

Substituting the given values:

$0.6 = \dfrac{1.8 \times 0.6 \times 1000}{M_2 \times 30}$

$0.6 = \dfrac{1.8 \times 20}{M_2}$

$0.6 = \dfrac{36}{M_2}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{36}{0.6} = 60 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$

Answer: $60 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$.

</details>

---

### Q6. MHT CET 2026 (19 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the osmotic pressure of a solution containing $3.42 \text{ g}$ of fructose (molar mass = $180 \text{ g/mol}$) in 1 litre of water at $400 \text{ K}$.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.624 \text{ atm}$
- **(B)** $0.180 \text{ atm}$
- **(C)** $0.340 \text{ atm}$
- **(D)** $0.420 \text{ atm}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $0.624 \text{ atm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure $\pi$ is given by the formula:

$\pi = C R T = \dfrac{n}{V} R T$

Given mass of fructose, $W = 3.42 \text{ g}$
Molar mass of fructose, $M = 180 \text{ g/mol}$
Volume of solution, $V = 1 \text{ L}$
Temperature, $T = 400 \text{ K}$
Gas constant, $R = 0.0821 \text{ L atm K}^{-1} \text{ mol}^{-1}$

Number of moles of fructose, $n = \dfrac{W}{M} = \dfrac{3.42}{180} = 0.019 \text{ mol}$

Substituting the values into the osmotic pressure formula:

$\pi = \dfrac{0.019}{1} \times 0.0821 \times 400$

$\pi = 0.019 \times 32.84 = 0.62396 \text{ atm}$

Rounding off, we get $\pi \approx 0.624 \text{ atm}$

Answer: $0.624 \text{ atm}$

</details>

### Q7. MHT CET 2026 (19 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Identify from following equimolar solutions that has maximum boiling point elevation. (Assuming complete dissociation)

**Options:**

- **(A)** Potassium chloride
- **(B)** Lithium sulfate
- **(C)** Aluminium sulphate .
- **(D)** Ammonium phosphate

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) Aluminium sulphate .**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is a colligative property and is given by the expression $\Delta T_b = i K_b m$.

For equimolar solutions, the molality $m$ is constant. Therefore, the elevation in boiling point is directly proportional to the van't Hoff factor $i$ (number of ions produced per formula unit upon complete dissociation).

Calculating the van't Hoff factor $i$ for each compound assuming complete dissociation:

Potassium chloride ($KCl$) $\rightarrow K^+ + Cl^-$ ($i = 2$)

Lithium sulfate ($Li_2SO_4$) $\rightarrow 2Li^+ + SO_4^{2-}$ ($i = 3$)

Aluminium sulphate ($Al_2(SO_4)_3$) $\rightarrow 2Al^{3+} + 3SO_4^{2-}$ ($i = 5$)

Ammonium phosphate ($(NH_4)_3PO_4$) $\rightarrow 3NH_4^+ + PO_4^{3-}$ ($i = 4$)

Since Aluminium sulphate has the maximum value of $i$, it produces the maximum number of particles in solution and thus exhibits the maximum boiling point elevation.

Answer: Aluminium sulphate .

</details>

---

### Q8. MHT CET 2026 (15 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the boiling point elevation of an aqueous solution containing $0.5$ g glucose in $20$ g water if $K_b$ for water is $0.52$ $\text{K kg mol}^{-1}$. [Molar mass of glucose $= 180$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.072\,K$
- **(B)** $0.054\,K$
- **(C)** $0.091\,K$
- **(D)** $1.08\,K$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $0.072\,K$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

where $m$ is the molality of the solution.

Molality $m = \dfrac{W_B \times 1000}{M_B \times W_A}$

Given:
$W_B = 0.5$ g (mass of glucose)
$M_B = 180$ g mol$^{-1}$ (molar mass of glucose)
$W_A = 20$ g (mass of water)
$K_b = 0.52$ K kg mol$^{-1}$

Substituting the values into the molality formula:

$m = \dfrac{0.5 \times 1000}{180 \times 20} = \dfrac{500}{3600} = \dfrac{5}{36}$ mol kg$^{-1}$

Now, calculating the boiling point elevation:

$\Delta T_b = 0.52 \times \dfrac{5}{36} = \dfrac{2.6}{36} \approx 0.072$ K

Answer: $0.072\,K$

</details>

---

### Q9. MHT CET 2026 (15 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate van't Hoff factor for $\text{CaCl}_2$, if osmotic pressure of $\text{CaCl}_2$ and urea solutions of same concentrations at same temperature are $0.65$ and $0.27$ atm respectively.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.95$
- **(B)** $2.1$
- **(C)** $2.9$
- **(D)** $2.41$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $2.41$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure of a solution is given by the formula $\pi = iCRT$, where $i$ is the van't Hoff factor, $C$ is the concentration, $R$ is the gas constant, and $T$ is the temperature.

For urea (a non-electrolyte), the van't Hoff factor $i = 1$.

$\pi_{\text{urea}} = 1 \times CRT = 0.27$

For $\text{CaCl}_2$ solution, the osmotic pressure is given as $0.65$ atm.

$\pi_{\text{CaCl}_2} = i \times CRT = 0.65$

Dividing the equation for $\text{CaCl}_2$ by the equation for urea:

$\dfrac{\pi_{\text{CaCl}_2}}{\pi_{\text{urea}}} = \dfrac{i \times CRT}{1 \times CRT}$

$\dfrac{0.65}{0.27} = i$

$i = \dfrac{65}{27} \approx 2.41$

Answer: $2.41$

</details>

---

### Q10. MHT CET 2026 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate the percent of dissociation of $0.01$m aqueous solution of $\text{K}_3[\text{Fe(CN)}_6]$ that freezes at $-0.062^\circ$C. [$K_f = 1.8$ K kg $\text{mol}^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $81.47\%$
- **(B)** $83.51\%$
- **(C)** $79.15\%$
- **(D)** $85.61\%$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $81.47\%$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$

Substituting the given values:

$0.062 = i \times 1.8 \times 0.01$

$i = \dfrac{0.062}{0.018} \approx 3.444$

The dissociation of potassium ferricyanide in aqueous solution is:

$\text{K}_3[\text{Fe(CN)}_6] \rightarrow 3\text{K}^+ + [\text{Fe(CN)}_6]^{3-}$

The number of ions produced per formula unit, $n = 4$.

The relationship between the van't Hoff factor ($i$) and the degree of dissociation ($\alpha$) is:

$i = 1 + (n - 1)\alpha$

$3.444 = 1 + (4 - 1)\alpha$

$3.444 = 1 + 3\alpha$

$3\alpha = 2.444$

$\alpha = \dfrac{2.444}{3} \approx 0.8147$

Therefore, the percent of dissociation is $81.47\%$.

Answer: $81.47\%$

</details>

### Q11. MHT CET 2026 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Which of following is the colligative property of a solution?

**Options:**

- **(A)** Vapour pressure
- **(B)** Boiling point
- **(C)** Osmotic pressure
- **(D)** Freezing point

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) Osmotic pressure**

**Detailed Solution:**

Colligative properties are those properties of dilute solutions which depend only on the number of solute particles and not on their nature.

The four important colligative properties are:
1. Relative lowering of vapour pressure
2. Elevation of boiling point
3. Depression of freezing point
4. Osmotic pressure

Vapour pressure, boiling point, and freezing point are not colligative properties by themselves; rather, their changes (relative lowering, elevation, and depression respectively) are colligative properties. Osmotic pressure is directly a colligative property.

Answer: Osmotic pressure

</details>

---

### Q12. MHT CET 2026 (14 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The osmotic pressure of a particular solution is $8$ atm at $298$ K. What will be the osmotic pressure under similar conditions at $313$ K?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $7.60$ atm
- **(B)** $11.65$ atm
- **(C)** $8.40$ atm
- **(D)** $4.84$ atm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $8.40$ atm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure of a solution is given by $\pi = CRT$.

For a given solution under similar conditions, the concentration $C$ remains constant. Therefore, the osmotic pressure is directly proportional to the absolute temperature, $\pi \propto T$.

$\dfrac{\pi_1}{T_1} = \dfrac{\pi_2}{T_2}$

Substituting the given values:

$\dfrac{8}{298} = \dfrac{\pi_2}{313}$

$\pi_2 = \dfrac{8 \times 313}{298}$

$\pi_2 = \dfrac{2504}{298} = 8.40$ atm

Answer: $8.40$ atm

</details>

### Q13. MHT CET 2026 (14 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A solution of $10.0$ g of a nonvolatile, nonelectrolyte solute dissolved in $0.2$ kg of benzene boils at $81.2^\circ$C. Calculate molar mass of the compound. (ebullioscopic constant for benzene $= 2.53$ K kg mol$^{-1}$ and boiling point of pure benzene $= 80.1^\circ$C)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $215$ gm/mol
- **(B)** $115$ gm/mol
- **(C)** $225$ gm/mol
- **(D)** $155$ gm/mol

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $115$ gm/mol**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given:
$w_2 = 10.0$ g
$w_1 = 0.2$ kg
$T_b = 81.2^\circ$C
$T_b^\circ = 80.1^\circ$C
$K_b = 2.53$ K kg mol$^{-1}$

Elevation in boiling point is given by:
$\Delta T_b = T_b - T_b^\circ = 81.2 - 80.1 = 1.1$ K

Using the formula for elevation in boiling point:
$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$
$\Delta T_b = K_b \times \dfrac{w_2}{M_2 \times w_1}$

Substituting the given values:
$1.1 = 2.53 \times \dfrac{10.0}{M_2 \times 0.2}$

$1.1 = \dfrac{2.53 \times 50}{M_2}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{126.5}{1.1} = 115$ g mol$^{-1}$

Answer: $115$ gm/mol

</details>

---

### Q14. MHT CET 2026 (14 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If a solution containing $10$ g per $\text{dm}^3$ of urea (molecular mass $= 60\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$) is isotonic with $5\%$ solution of a nonvolatile solute, find the molar mass of a nonvolatile solute.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $200\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $250\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $300\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $350\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $300\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Concentration of urea solution, $C_1 = \dfrac{10}{60}$ mol $\text{dm}^{-3}$

A $5\%$ solution of the nonvolatile solute means $5$ g of solute is present in $100$ mL (or $0.1$ $\text{dm}^3$) of the solution.

Concentration of the nonvolatile solute solution, $C_2 = \dfrac{5}{M \times 0.1} = \dfrac{50}{M}$ mol $\text{dm}^{-3}$

For isotonic solutions, the molar concentrations are equal, so $C_1 = C_2$

$\dfrac{10}{60} = \dfrac{50}{M}$

$M = \dfrac{50 \times 60}{10} = 300$ g $\text{mol}^{-1}$

Answer: $300\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q15. MHT CET 2026 (14 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Which of the following statements is TRUE regarding osmotic pressure?

**Options:**

- **(A)** It is directly proportional to molar mass of solvent.
- **(B)** It is directly proportational to the concentration of solution.
- **(C)** It is inversely proportional to number of moles of solute in solution.
- **(D)** It is inversely proportional to tempertaure.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) It is directly proportational to the concentration of solution.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Osmotic pressure is a colligative property given by the expression $\pi = CRT$, where $C$ is the molar concentration of the solution, $R$ is the gas constant, and $T$ is the absolute temperature.

From this expression, it is evident that osmotic pressure is directly proportional to the concentration of the solution ($C$) at a given temperature.

Since $C = \dfrac{n}{V}$, where $n$ is the number of moles of solute, osmotic pressure is directly proportional to the number of moles of solute.

It is also directly proportional to the absolute temperature ($T$).

Thus, the only true statement is that osmotic pressure is directly proportional to the concentration of the solution.

Answer: It is directly proportational to the concentration of solution.

</details>

---

### Q16. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the mass of solute dissolved in $100$ g solvent that produces boiling point elevation $0.5$ K.
[$K_b$ for solvent $= 2$ $\text{K kg mol}^{-1}$ $\&$ Molar mass of solute $=120$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2$ g.
- **(B)** $3$ g.
- **(C)** $4$ g.
- **(D)** $5$ g.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $3$ g.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the formula for elevation in boiling point:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

$\Delta T_b = \dfrac{1000 \times K_b \times W_B}{M \times W_A}$

Substituting the given values:

$0.5 = \dfrac{1000 \times 2 \times W_B}{120 \times 100}$

$0.5 = \dfrac{2000 \times W_B}{12000}$

$0.5 = \dfrac{W_B}{6}$

$W_B = 3$ g

Answer: $3$ g.

</details>

---

### Q17. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The relative lowering of vapor pressure obtained by dissolving $72$ g of solute in $1000$ g of water is $7.2 \times 10^{-3}$. Determine the molar mass of the solute

**Options:**

- **(A)** $158$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $180$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $192$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $164$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $180$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The relative lowering of vapor pressure is given by Raoult's law:

$\dfrac{\Delta P}{P^0} = \dfrac{n_2}{n_1 + n_2}$

For a dilute solution, $n_1 + n_2 \approx n_1$. The equation simplifies to:

$\dfrac{\Delta P}{P^0} = \dfrac{n_2}{n_1} = \dfrac{w_2 \times M_1}{M_2 \times w_1}$

Given:
Relative lowering of vapor pressure, $\dfrac{\Delta P}{P^0} = 7.2 \times 10^{-3}$
Mass of solute, $w_2 = 72 \text{ g}$
Mass of solvent (water), $w_1 = 1000 \text{ g}$
Molar mass of solvent (water), $M_1 = 18 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$

Substituting the values into the formula:

$7.2 \times 10^{-3} = \dfrac{72 \times 18}{M_2 \times 1000}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{72 \times 18}{1000 \times 7.2 \times 10^{-3}}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{72 \times 18}{7.2}$

$M_2 = 10 \times 18 = 180 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$

Answer: $180$ $\text{g mol}^{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q18. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A $0.1$ molal aqueous solution of $\text{K}_3[\text{Fe(CN)}_6]$ freezes at $-0.85\,^\circ$C. If $K_f$ for water is $1.86$ K kg mol$^{-1}$, what is the van't Hoff factor for this solution?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3.0$
- **(B)** $3.5$
- **(C)** $4.0$
- **(D)** $4.57$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $4.57$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by the equation:

$\Delta T_f = i \cdot K_f \cdot m$

From the given data:

$\Delta T_f = 0.85$

$K_f = 1.86$

$m = 0.1$

Substituting the values into the equation:

$0.85 = i \cdot 1.86 \cdot 0.1$

$i = \dfrac{0.85}{0.186}$

$i \approx 4.57$

Answer: $4.57$

</details>

### Q19. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

What is the molar mass of a solute when a solution is prepared by dissolving $0.8$ g of solute in $0.3$ dm$^3$ of water having osmotic pressure $0.2$ atm at $300$ K (R = $0.082$ atm dm$^3$ K$^{-1}$ mol$^{-1}$)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $300$ g/mol
- **(B)** $280$ g/mol
- **(C)** $328$ g/mol
- **(D)** $345$ g/mol

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $328$ g/mol**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the formula for osmotic pressure $\pi = \dfrac{W}{M V} R T$

Rearranging for molar mass $M$:

$M = \dfrac{W R T}{\pi V}$

Substituting the given values:

$M = \dfrac{0.8 \times 0.082 \times 300}{0.2 \times 0.3}$

$M = \dfrac{0.8}{0.2} \times \dfrac{300}{0.3} \times 0.082$

$M = 4 \times 1000 \times 0.082$

$M = 328$ g/mol

Answer: $328$ g/mol

</details>

### Q20. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $\Delta T_b$ of $0.5$ m urea solution is $x\,^0$C find the expected value of $\Delta T_b$ for $0.5$ m $\text{Al}_2(\text{SO}_4)_3$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2x\,^0$C
- **(B)** $3x\,^0$C
- **(C)** $5x\,^0$C
- **(D)** $4x\,^0$C

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $5x\,^0$C**

**Detailed Solution:**

For urea solution, the van 't Hoff factor $i = 1$.

$\Delta T_b = i \times K_b \times m$

$x = 1 \times K_b \times 0.5$

For $\text{Al}_2(\text{SO}_4)_3$ solution, assuming complete dissociation, it yields $2\text{Al}^{3+}$ and $3\text{SO}_4^{2-}$ ions, giving a van 't Hoff factor $i = 5$.

$\Delta T_b' = i \times K_b \times m$

$\Delta T_b' = 5 \times K_b \times 0.5$

$\Delta T_b' = 5x$

Answer: $5x\,^0$C

</details>

### Q21. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A $0.1$ M solution of a solute $A$ is Isotonic with a solution of $B$ containing $1.5$ gm of it per $100$ cm$^3$. What is the molar mass of $B$?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $150\,gmol^{-1}$
- **(B)** $115\,gmol^{-1}$
- **(C)** $75\,gmol^{-1}$
- **(D)** $750\,gmol^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $150\,gmol^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

For isotonic solutions, the osmotic pressures are equal, which implies their molar concentrations are equal at the same temperature.

$C_A = C_B$

Given $C_A = 0.1$ M

For solution B, mass of solute $W_B = 1.5$ g and volume of solution $V = 100$ cm$^3 = 0.1$ L.

$C_B = \dfrac{W_B}{M_B \times V} = \dfrac{1.5}{M_B \times 0.1} = \dfrac{15}{M_B}$

Equating the concentrations:

$0.1 = \dfrac{15}{M_B}$

$M_B = \dfrac{15}{0.1} = 150$ g mol$^{-1}$

Answer: $150\,gmol^{-1}$

</details>

### Q22. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate the boiling point of solution prepared by adding $2.93$ g of solute to $0.059$ kg of solvent, if the boiling point of pure solvent = $61.2^\circ C$, $K_b = 3.85^\circ C$ Kg mol$^{-1}$ and molar mass of solute $= 32$ g/mol)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $64.13\,^0 C$
- **(B)** $58.27\,^0 C$
- **(C)** $67.17\,^0 C$
- **(D)** $63.45\,^0 C$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $67.17\,^0 C$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

where $m$ is the molality of the solution.

Molality $m = \dfrac{w_2}{M_2 \times w_1}$

Given:
$w_2 = 2.93$ g
$M_2 = 32$ g/mol
$w_1 = 0.059$ kg
$K_b = 3.85^\circ$C kg mol$^{-1}$

Substituting the values:

$m = \dfrac{2.93}{32 \times 0.059} = \dfrac{2.93}{1.888} = 1.552$ mol kg$^{-1}$

$\Delta T_b = 3.85 \times 1.552 = 5.97^\circ$C

The boiling point of the solution $T_b$ is:

$T_b = T_b^\circ + \Delta T_b$

$T_b = 61.2^\circ$C $+ 5.97^\circ$C $= 67.17^\circ$C

Answer: $67.17\,^0 C$

</details>

---

### Q23. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the freezing point of a $0.05$ m aqueous solution of a non-electrolyte at $298$ K. ($K_f = 1.86$ K kg mol$^{-1}$)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $-1.86\,^\circ C$
- **(B)** $-0.93\,^\circ C$
- **(C)** $-0.093\,^\circ C$
- **(D)** $0.093\,^\circ C$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $-0.093\,^\circ C$**

**Detailed Solution:**

For a non-electrolyte, the van 't Hoff factor $i = 1$.

The depression in freezing point is given by:

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$

Substituting the given values:

$\Delta T_f = 1 \times 1.86 \times 0.05 = 0.093$ K

The freezing point of pure water is $0\,^\circ\text{C}$.

The freezing point of the solution is:

$T_f = T_f^\circ - \Delta T_f = 0 - 0.093 = -0.093\,^\circ\text{C}$

Answer: $-0.093\,^\circ C$

</details>

---

### Q24. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate the amount of KCl that should be added to $100$ g of pure water to decrease the freezing point of water by $0.3535$ K, if $K_f$ for water is $1.86$ K kg mol$^{-1}$.
[Degree of dissociation of KCl ($\alpha$) $= 0.9$, Molar mass of KCl $= 74.5$ g/mol]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $7.45$ g
- **(B)** $0.745$ g
- **(C)** $3.725$ g
- **(D)** $0.3725$ g

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $0.745$ g**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given $\Delta T_f = 0.3535$ K, $K_f = 1.86$ K kg mol$^{-1}$, $\alpha = 0.9$, $M_B = 74.5$ g/mol, $W_A = 100$ g $= 0.1$ kg.

For KCl, the number of ions $n = 2$.

The van't Hoff factor $i$ is given by:

$i = 1 + (n - 1)\alpha = 1 + (2 - 1) \times 0.9 = 1.9$

The depression in freezing point is given by:

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times \dfrac{W_B}{M_B \times W_A}$

Substituting the given values:

$0.3535 = 1.9 \times 1.86 \times \dfrac{W_B}{74.5 \times 0.1}$

$0.3535 = 3.534 \times \dfrac{W_B}{7.45}$

$W_B = \dfrac{0.3535 \times 7.45}{3.534} \approx 0.1 \times 7.45 = 0.745$ g

Answer: $0.745$ g

</details>

---

### Q25. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the temperature of the solution in which a solution containing $10$ g solute in $0.4$ dm$^3$ has an osmotic pressure of $3.4$ atm.
[Molar mass of solute $=180$ g mol$^{-1}$, $R = 0.082$ atm dm$^3$ K$^{-1}$mol$^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $25.5\,^\circ$C.
- **(B)** $20.2\,^\circ$C.
- **(C)** $27.5\,^\circ$C.
- **(D)** $30.7\,^\circ$C.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $25.5\,^\circ$C.**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure is given by the formula:

$\pi = C R T = \dfrac{w}{M V} R T$

Substituting the given values:

$3.4 = \dfrac{10}{180 \times 0.4} \times 0.082 \times T$

$T = \dfrac{3.4 \times 180 \times 0.4}{10 \times 0.082}$

$T = \dfrac{244.8}{0.82} = 298.53$ K

Converting the temperature to Celsius:

$T(^{\circ}\text{C}) = 298.53 - 273 = 25.53^{\circ}\text{C}$

Answer: $25.5\,^\circ$C.

</details>

---

### Q26. MHT CET 2026 (20 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Find the osmotic pressure of 0.01 M solution at 25 °C $(R = 0.0821\ \text{L atm mol}^{-1}\text{K}^{-1})$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.201$ atm
- **(B)** $0.245$ atm
- **(C)** $0.082$ atm
- **(D)** $0.41$ atm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $0.245$ atm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure $\pi$ is given by the formula:

$\pi = CRT$

Here, $C = 0.01$ M, $T = 25^\circ\text{C} = 298$ K, and $R = 0.0821$ L atm mol$^{-1}$ K$^{-1}$.

$\pi = 0.01 \times 0.0821 \times 298$

$\pi = 0.244658$ atm $\approx 0.245$ atm

Answer: $0.245$ atm

</details>

### Q27. MHT CET 2026 (20 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Identify the colligative property from the followings?

**Options:**

- **(A)** Vapour pressure of solution.
- **(B)** Osmotic pressure of solution.
- **(C)** Boiling point of solution.
- **(D)** Freezing point of solution.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) Osmotic pressure of solution.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Colligative properties depend only on the number of solute particles in a solution and not on their nature.

The four colligative properties are:
1. Relative lowering of vapour pressure
2. Elevation in boiling point
3. Depression in freezing point
4. Osmotic pressure

Vapour pressure, boiling point, and freezing point of a solution are not colligative properties themselves. Osmotic pressure of a solution is a colligative property.

Answer: Osmotic pressure of solution.

</details>

---

### Q29. MHT CET 2026 (20 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the mass of nonvolatile solute dissolved in $0.3$ dm$^3$ water having osmotic pressure $0.1$ atm at $300$K.
[Molar mass of solute = $328$ g mol$^{-1}$, R = $0.082$ dm$^3$atm K$^{-1}$mol$^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.4$ g
- **(B)** $0.6$ g
- **(C)** $0.8$ g
- **(D)** $1.0$ g

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $0.4$ g**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure is given by the formula:

$\pi = \dfrac{W}{M V} R T$

Rearranging for the mass of the solute $W$:

$W = \dfrac{\pi M V}{R T}$

Substituting the given values:

$W = \dfrac{0.1 \times 328 \times 0.3}{0.082 \times 300}$

$W = \dfrac{9.84}{24.6}$

$W = 0.4$ g

Answer: $0.4$ g

### Q30. MHT CET 2026 (20 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which from following solutions in water on complete dissociation exhibits minimum freezing point depression?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.2$ m Potassium chloride
- **(B)** $0.1$ m Sodium chloride
- **(C)** $0.05$ m Aluminium phosphate
- **(D)** $0.15$ m Magnesium sulphate

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $0.05$ m Aluminium phosphate**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by $\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$.

For complete dissociation, the van't Hoff factor $i$ is equal to the number of ions produced per formula unit.

For $0.2$ m Potassium chloride (KCl): $i = 2$, $i \times m = 2 \times 0.2 = 0.4$

For $0.1$ m Sodium chloride (NaCl): $i = 2$, $i \times m = 2 \times 0.1 = 0.2$

For $0.05$ m Aluminium phosphate (AlPO$_4$): $i = 2$, $i \times m = 2 \times 0.05 = 0.1$

For $0.15$ m Magnesium sulphate (MgSO$_4$): $i = 2$, $i \times m = 2 \times 0.15 = 0.3$

Since $0.05$ m Aluminium phosphate has the minimum value of $i \times m$, it exhibits the minimum freezing point depression.

Answer: $0.05$ m Aluminium phosphate

</details>

### Q31. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate the molality of aqueous solution of electrolyte that freezes at $-0.93\,^\circ\text{C}$ if $K_f$ for water and van't Hoff factor respectively are $1.86\,\text{K kg mol}^{-1}$ and $1.25$. (Freezing point of water $= 0\,^\circ\text{C}$ )

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.2$ m
- **(B)** $0.5$ m
- **(C)** $0.3$ m
- **(D)** $0.4$ m

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $0.4$ m**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$

Given:

Freezing point of solution, $T_f = -0.93\,^\circ\text{C}$

Freezing point of water, $T_f^\circ = 0\,^\circ\text{C}$

$\Delta T_f = T_f^\circ - T_f = 0 - (-0.93) = 0.93\,^\circ\text{C}$

van't Hoff factor, $i = 1.25$

Cryoscopic constant, $K_f = 1.86\,\text{K kg mol}^{-1}$

Substituting the values into the formula:

$0.93 = 1.25 \times 1.86 \times m$

$m = \dfrac{0.93}{1.25 \times 1.86}$

$m = \dfrac{0.5}{1.25} = 0.4\,\text{m}$

Answer: $0.4$ m

</details>

### Q32. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which among the following pairs of solutions is isotonic in nature?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.1$ M urea and $0.1$ M sucrose
- **(B)** $0.1$ M urea and $0.1$ M KCl
- **(C)** $0.1$ M sucrose and $0.1$ M KCl
- **(D)** $0.1$ M KCl and $0.1$ M $\text{CaCl}_2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $0.1$ M urea and $0.1$ M sucrose**

**Detailed Solution:**

Isotonic solutions are those which have the same osmotic pressure at a given temperature.

Osmotic pressure is given by $\pi = iCRT$, where $i$ is the van't Hoff factor, $C$ is the molar concentration, $R$ is the gas constant, and $T$ is the temperature.

For two solutions to be isotonic, their effective concentrations must be equal: $i_1C_1 = i_2C_2$.

For $0.1$ M urea and $0.1$ M sucrose:
Urea is a non-electrolyte, so $i = 1$. Effective concentration $= 1 \times 0.1 = 0.1$ M.
Sucrose is a non-electrolyte, so $i = 1$. Effective concentration $= 1 \times 0.1 = 0.1$ M.
Since $i_1C_1 = i_2C_2$, these two solutions are isotonic.

For $0.1$ M urea and $0.1$ M KCl:
KCl dissociates into $K^+$ and $Cl^-$, so $i = 2$. Effective concentration $= 2 \times 0.1 = 0.2$ M.
They are not isotonic.

For $0.1$ M KCl and $0.1$ M $\text{CaCl}_2$:
$\text{CaCl}_2$ dissociates into $Ca^{2+}$ and $2Cl^-$, so $i = 3$. Effective concentration $= 3 \times 0.1 = 0.3$ M.
They are not isotonic.

Answer: $0.1$ M urea and $0.1$ M sucrose

</details>

---

### Q33. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

A solution of non volatile solute has boiling point elevation $0.70$ K if $K_b$ for the solvent is $2.44\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$. What is the molality of the solution?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3.48$ m
- **(B)** $2.86$ m
- **(C)** $0.286$ m
- **(D)** $0.0186$ m

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $0.286$ m**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

where $\Delta T_b$ is the boiling point elevation, $K_b$ is the ebullioscopic constant, and $m$ is the molality of the solution.

Given:
$\Delta T_b = 0.70$ K
$K_b = 2.44$ K kg mol$^{-1}$

Substituting the given values into the formula:

$0.70 = 2.44 \times m$

$m = \dfrac{0.70}{2.44}$

$m \approx 0.286$ m

Answer: $0.286$ m

</details>

### Q34. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

van't Hoff factor for $\text{BaCl}_2$ is $2.47$, calculate the percentage dissociation of in its aqueous solution.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $25.6\%$
- **(B)** $35.7\%$
- **(C)** $73.5\%$
- **(D)** $13.6\%$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $73.5\%$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The dissociation reaction of $\text{BaCl}_2$ is:

$\text{BaCl}_2 \rightleftharpoons \text{Ba}^{2+} + 2\text{Cl}^{-}$

The number of ions produced per formula unit, $n = 3$.

The relation between van't Hoff factor $i$ and degree of dissociation $\alpha$ is:

$i = 1 + (n - 1)\alpha$

Substituting the given values:

$2.47 = 1 + (3 - 1)\alpha$

$2.47 = 1 + 2\alpha$

$2\alpha = 1.47$

$\alpha = 0.735$

Percentage dissociation $= \alpha \times 100 = 73.5\%$

</details>

### Q35. MHT CET 2026 (18 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate the molar mass of nonelectrolyte solute when 6 gram of it is dissolved in 1 $\text{dm}^3$ water has osmotic pressure $2.4\ \text{atm}$ at 300 K $(R = 0.0821\ \text{atm dm}^3\ \text{K}^{-1}\ \text{mol}^{-1})$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $45.0\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $74.12\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $61.58\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $90.28\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $61.58\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure is given by the formula:

$\pi = C R T = \dfrac{W R T}{M V}$

Rearranging the formula to solve for the molar mass $M$:

$M = \dfrac{W R T}{\pi V}$

Substituting the given values $W = 6\ \text{g}$, $R = 0.0821\ \text{atm dm}^3\ \text{K}^{-1}\ \text{mol}^{-1}$, $T = 300\ \text{K}$, $\pi = 2.4\ \text{atm}$, and $V = 1\ \text{dm}^3$:

$M = \dfrac{6 \times 0.0821 \times 300}{2.4 \times 1}$

$M = \dfrac{147.78}{2.4}$

$M = 61.575\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$

Rounding to two decimal places, the molar mass is $61.58\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$.

Answer: $61.58\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$

</details>

### Q36. MHT CET 2026 (18 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

$40$ gram nonelectrolyte solute having molar mass $180\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$ dissolved in water has osmotic pressure $2\ \text{atm}$ at $300\ \text{K}$. Calculate the volume of solution. ($\text{R} = 0.0821\ \text{atm mol}^{-1}\text{K}^{-1}$)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2.10\ \text{dm}^3$
- **(B)** $2.34\ \text{dm}^3$
- **(C)** $2.74\ \text{dm}^3$
- **(D)** $3.40\ \text{dm}^3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $2.74\ \text{dm}^3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the formula for osmotic pressure:

$\pi = \dfrac{n}{V}RT$

Rearranging for volume $V$:

$V = \dfrac{nRT}{\pi}$

Number of moles of solute $n = \dfrac{40}{180} = \dfrac{2}{9}\ \text{mol}$

Substituting the given values:

$V = \dfrac{\dfrac{2}{9} \times 0.0821 \times 300}{2}$

$V = \dfrac{24.63}{9}$

$V = 2.736\ \text{L}$

Since $1\ \text{L} = 1\ \text{dm}^3$, the volume of the solution is $2.74\ \text{dm}^3$.

Answer: $2.74\ \text{dm}^3$

</details>

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the molality of solution of nonvolatile solute if boiling point of solution, molal elevation constant for solvent are $319.8\ \text{K}$ and $2.5\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$ respectively [Boiling point of solvent = $319.5\ \text{K}$]

Substituting the given values:

$\Delta T_b = 319.8 - 319.5 = 0.3\ \text{K}$

The relation between elevation in boiling point and molality is $\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

Rearranging for molality $m$:

$m = \dfrac{\Delta T_b}{K_b}$

$m = \dfrac{0.3}{2.5} = 0.12\ \text{mol kg}^{-1}$

### Q38. MHT CET 2026 (18 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Identify correct statements from following regarding $0.2\ \text{M}$ urea and $0.2\ \text{M}$ sucrose solutions.

**Options:**

- **(A)** The urea solution is hypertonic to sucrose solution.
- **(B)** The osmotic pressure of urea solution is higher than that of sucrose solution.
- **(C)** These solutions exhibit same osmotic pressure.
- **(D)** The sucrose solution is hypotonic to urea solution.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) These solutions exhibit same osmotic pressure.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Osmotic pressure is given by $\pi = iCRT$.

For urea, $i = 1$ and $C = 0.2\ \text{M}$.

For sucrose, $i = 1$ and $C = 0.2\ \text{M}$.

Since both solutions have the same concentration and the same van 't Hoff factor, their osmotic pressures are equal at a given temperature.

Therefore, these solutions exhibit the same osmotic pressure and are isotonic to each other.

Answer: These solutions exhibit same osmotic pressure.

</details>

### Q39. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate the molar mass of a nonvolatile solute if 6.4 g of it dissolved in 100 g water produces a relative lowering in vapour pressure of $0 \cdot 016$ at 300 K.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $60 \text{ gmol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $66 \text{ gmol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $72 \text{ gmol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $84 \text{ gmol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $72 \text{ gmol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The relative lowering in vapour pressure is given by Raoult's law:

$\dfrac{\Delta P}{P^0} = \dfrac{n_2}{n_1 + n_2}$

For a dilute solution, $n_2 \ll n_1$, so $n_1 + n_2 \approx n_1$. The equation simplifies to:

$\dfrac{\Delta P}{P^0} \approx \dfrac{n_2}{n_1} = \dfrac{w_2 M_1}{M_2 w_1}$

Given:
Relative lowering in vapour pressure, $\dfrac{\Delta P}{P^0} = 0.016$
Mass of solute, $w_2 = 6.4 \text{ g}$
Mass of solvent (water), $w_1 = 100 \text{ g}$
Molar mass of water, $M_1 = 18 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$

Substituting the values into the formula:

$0.016 = \dfrac{6.4 \times 18}{M_2 \times 100}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{6.4 \times 18}{0.016 \times 100}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{115.2}{1.6} = 72 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$

Answer: $72 \text{ gmol}^{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q40. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the cryoscopic constant of a solvent if depression in freezing point of $0 \cdot 3$ m solution of nonelectrolyte is $0 \cdot 48$ K

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1 \cdot 1 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $1 \cdot 6 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $2 \cdot 2 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $2 \cdot 6 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $1 \cdot 6 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$

For a nonelectrolyte, the van't Hoff factor $i = 1$.

Substituting the given values:

$0 \cdot 48 = 1 \times K_f \times 0 \cdot 3$

$K_f = \dfrac{0 \cdot 48}{0 \cdot 3} = 1 \cdot 6 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$

Answer: $1 \cdot 6 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q41. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Identify the correct statement from following.

**Options:**

- **(A)** The vapour pressure of a solvent increases by dissolving nonvolatile solute into it.
- **(B)** The boiling point of solvent decreases by dissolving a nonvolatile solute into it.
- **(C)** The osmotic pressure of electrolytic solution is greater than nonelectrolytic solution of the same concentration.
- **(D)** The freezing point of solvent is a colligative properly.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) The osmotic pressure of electrolytic solution is greater than nonelectrolytic solution of the same concentration.**

**Detailed Solution:**

When a non-volatile solute is added to a solvent, the vapour pressure of the solvent decreases. This is known as the relative lowering of vapour pressure.

Due to the lowering of vapour pressure, the boiling point of the solution increases. This is known as the elevation in boiling point.

Osmotic pressure is a colligative property, meaning it depends on the number of solute particles in the solution. Electrolytes dissociate into ions in solution, resulting in a van't Hoff factor $i > 1$. Non-electrolytes do not dissociate, so $i = 1$. Since osmotic pressure $\pi = iCRT$, an electrolytic solution will have a greater osmotic pressure than a non-electrolytic solution of the same concentration.

The depression in freezing point is a colligative property, not the freezing point itself.

Answer: The osmotic pressure of electrolytic solution is greater than nonelectrolytic solution of the same concentration.

</details>

---

### Q42. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Which of the following concentrations of solutions of urea in water exhibits minimum freezing point depression?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.12$ m
- **(B)** $0.18$ m
- **(C)** $0.08$ m
- **(D)** $0.06$ m

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $0.06$ m**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by $\Delta T_f = i \cdot K_f \cdot m$.

For urea, being a non-electrolyte, the van 't Hoff factor $i = 1$.

Thus, $\Delta T_f \propto m$.

To exhibit the minimum freezing point depression, the solution must have the minimum molality.

Among the given options, $0.06$ m is the minimum concentration.

Therefore, the $0.06$ m urea solution exhibits the minimum freezing point depression.

Answer: $0.06$ m

</details>

### Q43. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate van't Hoff factor for aqueous solution of 0.02 m formic acid if it freezes at -0.045 $^\circ\text{C}$.
[ $K_f$ = 1.86 K kg $\text{mol}^{-1}$ and freezing point of water = $0\ ^\circ\text{C}$ ]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.21$
- **(B)** $1.46$
- **(C)** $1.68$
- **(D)** $1.05$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $1.21$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by $\Delta T_f = T_f^\circ - T_f$.

$\Delta T_f = 0 - (-0.045) = 0.045 \text{ K}$

Using the formula for depression in freezing point:

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$

Substituting the given values:

$0.045 = i \times 1.86 \times 0.02$

$0.045 = i \times 0.0372$

$i = \dfrac{0.045}{0.0372} = 1.209$

$i \approx 1.21$

Answer: $1.21$

</details>

### Q44. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the molar mass of nonvolatile solute when 4 g of it is dissolved in 100 g solvent that boils at 319.4 K  [$K_b$ = 2.4 K kg $\text{mol}^{-1}$; B.P of pure solvent = 319 K]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $220\ \text{gmol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $230\ \text{gmol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $240\ \text{gmol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $250\ \text{gmol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $240\ \text{gmol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Elevation in boiling point is given by $\Delta T_b = T_b - T_b^0$

$\Delta T_b = 319.4 - 319 = 0.4$ K

Using the formula for elevation in boiling point:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m = K_b \times \dfrac{w_2 \times 1000}{M_2 \times w_1}$

Substituting the given values:

$0.4 = 2.4 \times \dfrac{4 \times 1000}{M_2 \times 100}$

$0.4 = \dfrac{96}{M_2}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{96}{0.4} = 240$ g mol$^{-1}$

Answer: $240\ \text{gmol}^{-1}$

</details>

### Q45. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The normal boiling point of Ethyl acetate is $77^\circ\text{C}$. A solution of non-volatile non-electrolyte solute in ethyl acetate boils at $78^\circ\text{C}$. $K_b$ for ethyl acetate is $2.77\ ^\circ\text{C kg mol}^{-1}$. What is the molality of the solution ?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.361\ \text{m}$
- **(B)** $0.052\ \text{m}$
- **(C)** $0.075\ \text{m}$
- **(D)** $0.25\ \text{m}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $0.361\ \text{m}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by $\Delta T_b = T_b - T_b^{\circ}$

Substituting the given values:

$\Delta T_b = 78^{\circ}\text{C} - 77^{\circ}\text{C} = 1^{\circ}\text{C}$

For a non-volatile, non-electrolyte solute, the elevation in boiling point is related to molality $m$ by the formula $\Delta T_b = K_b m$

$1 = 2.77 m$

$m = \dfrac{1}{2.77} = 0.361\ \text{m}$

Answer: $0.361\ \text{m}$

</details>

### Q46. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

18 g of glucose (molar mass = 180 g/mol) is dissolved in water to prepare 500 ml solution at $15^\circ\text{C}$. Calculate the osmotic pressure of the solution. [$R = 0.0821\ \text{L atm K}^{-1}\ \text{mol}^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.65\ \text{atm}$
- **(B)** $4.73\ \text{atm}$
- **(C)** $5.57\ \text{atm}$
- **(D)** $2.34\ \text{atm}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $4.73\ \text{atm}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Number of moles of glucose, $n = \dfrac{18}{180} = 0.1\text{ mol}$

Volume of solution, $V = 500\text{ ml} = 0.5\text{ L}$

Temperature, $T = 15 + 273 = 288\text{ K}$

Osmotic pressure is given by $\pi = CRT = \dfrac{n}{V}RT$

Substituting the values:

$\pi = \dfrac{0.1}{0.5} \times 0.0821 \times 288$

$\pi = 0.2 \times 0.0821 \times 288$

$\pi = 4.72896\text{ atm} \approx 4.73\text{ atm}$

Answer: $4.73\ \text{atm}$

</details>

### Q47. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 1)

[Molar mass of glucose $= 180\ \text{g mol}^{-1}$ and boiling point of water $= 100\ ^\circ\text{C}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $100\ ^\circ\text{C}$

Mass of water $= 100\ \text{g} = 0.1\ \text{kg}$

Molality of the solution, $m = \dfrac{0.1}{0.1} = 1\ \text{mol kg}^{-1}$

Elevation in boiling point, $\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

$\Delta T_b = 0.5 \times 1 = 0.5\ ^\circ\text{C}$

### Q48. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Which of the following is the colligative property of a solution?

**Options:**

- **(A)** vapour pressure
- **(B)** boiling point
- **(C)** osmotic pressure
- **(D)** Freezing point

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) osmotic pressure**

**Detailed Solution:**

Colligative properties are those properties of a solution that depend only on the number of solute particles and not on their nature.

The four colligative properties are:
1. Relative lowering of vapour pressure
2. Elevation of boiling point
3. Depression of freezing point
4. Osmotic pressure

Vapour pressure, boiling point, and freezing point are not colligative properties themselves; rather, their changes (lowering, elevation, and depression, respectively) are colligative properties. Therefore, osmotic pressure is the only colligative property among the given options.

Answer: osmotic pressure

</details>

---

### Q49. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If a 0.4 molal solution of a nonvolatile solute in organic solvent decreases its freezing point by 2.4 K., then the molal depression constant of solvent will be

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3.0\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $4.0\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $5.0\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $6.0\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $6.0\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the formula for depression in freezing point:

$\Delta T_f = K_f \times m$

Given $\Delta T_f = 2.4$ K and $m = 0.4$ mol kg$^{-1}$.

Substituting the values:

$2.4 = K_f \times 0.4$

$K_f = \dfrac{2.4}{0.4} = 6.0$ K kg mol$^{-1}$

Answer: $6.0\ \text{K kg mol}^{-1}$

</details>

### Q50. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which of the following colligative properties is useful to determine the molar masses of proteins, polymers or colloids, with the greatest precision?

**Options:**

- **(A)** Elevation in boiling point
- **(B)** Depression in freezing point
- **(C)** Osmotic pressure
- **(D)** Relative lowering of vapour pressure

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) Osmotic pressure**

**Detailed Solution:**

Proteins, polymers and colloids have very high molar masses. Due to this, their solutions have very low molar concentrations.

The magnitude of colligative properties such as elevation in boiling point, depression in freezing point and relative lowering of vapour pressure is extremely small for such dilute solutions and cannot be measured with high precision.

Osmotic pressure provides a measurable value even for very dilute solutions. Furthermore, osmotic pressure measurements are carried out at room temperature, which prevents the denaturation or degradation of sensitive biomolecules like proteins.

Therefore, osmotic pressure is the most useful colligative property to determine the molar masses of macromolecules with the greatest precision.

Answer: Osmotic pressure

</details>

---

### Q51. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate the van't Hoff factor of a centimolar solution of potassium ferrocyanide if it is $60\%$ dissociated at $300$ K.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3.4$
- **(B)** $3.9$
- **(C)** $3.1$
- **(D)** $3.7$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $3.4$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The chemical formula of potassium ferrocyanide is $K_4[Fe(CN)_6]$.

The dissociation reaction is:
$K_4[Fe(CN)_6] \rightleftharpoons 4K^+ + [Fe(CN)_6]^{4-}$

The number of ions produced per formula unit, $n = 5$.

The degree of dissociation, $\alpha = 60\% = 0.60$.

The van't Hoff factor $i$ is given by:
$i = 1 + (n - 1)\alpha$

Substituting the values:
$i = 1 + (5 - 1) \times 0.60$

$i = 1 + 4 \times 0.60$

$i = 1 + 2.4 = 3.4$

Answer: $3.4$

</details>

---

### Q52. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

$5$ g urea is dissolved in $100$ g water. Find the amount of glucose to be dissolved in $120$ g of water so that the boiling points of both solutions will be the same.
[Molar mass of urea $= 60$ g $\text{mol}^{-1}$ & Molar mass of glucose $= 180$ g $\text{mol}^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $17$ g
- **(B)** $19$ g
- **(C)** $18$ g
- **(D)** $20$ g

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $18$ g**

**Detailed Solution:**

Elevation in boiling point is given by $\Delta T_b = K_b m$

For the boiling points to be the same, the molality of both solutions must be equal.

Molality of urea solution $= \dfrac{W_{\text{urea}}}{M_{\text{urea}} \times W_{\text{water (kg)}}} = \dfrac{5}{60 \times 0.1} = \dfrac{5}{6}$ m

Let the mass of glucose be $x$ g.

Molality of glucose solution $= \dfrac{W_{\text{glucose}}}{M_{\text{glucose}} \times W_{\text{water (kg)}}} = \dfrac{x}{180 \times 0.12} = \dfrac{x}{21.6}$ m

Equating the molalities:

$\dfrac{5}{6} = \dfrac{x}{21.6}$

$x = \dfrac{5 \times 21.6}{6} = 5 \times 3.6 = 18$ g

Answer: $18$ g

</details>

### Q53. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which of the following solutes has the ratio of theoretical molar mass to the experimentally observed molar mass is $3$?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\text{KCl}$
- **(B)** $\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4$
- **(C)** $\text{MgSO}_4$
- **(D)** $\text{Al}_2(\text{SO}_4)_3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The ratio of theoretical molar mass to the experimentally observed molar mass is known as the van 't Hoff factor $i$.

$i = \dfrac{\text{Theoretical molar mass}}{\text{Observed molar mass}}$

We need to find the solute for which $i = 3$. Assuming complete dissociation for the given strong electrolytes:

For $\text{KCl} \rightarrow \text{K}^+ + \text{Cl}^-$, $i = 2$

For $\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4 \rightarrow 2\text{K}^+ + \text{SO}_4^{2-}$, $i = 3$

For $\text{MgSO}_4 \rightarrow \text{Mg}^{2+} + \text{SO}_4^{2-}$, $i = 2$

For $\text{Al}_2(\text{SO}_4)_3 \rightarrow 2\text{Al}^{3+} + 3\text{SO}_4^{2-}$, $i = 5$

Thus, $\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4$ has a van 't Hoff factor of $3$.

Answer: $\text{K}_2\text{SO}_4$

</details>

### Q54. MHT CET 2026 (13 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

An aqueous solution has an osmotic pressure of $4.92$ atm at $300$ K.
Calculate the molarity of the solution.
($R = 0.082$ L atm mol$^{-1}$ K$^{-1}$)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.1$ M
- **(B)** $0.2$ M
- **(C)** $0.25$ M
- **(D)** $0.4$ M

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $0.2$ M**

**Detailed Solution:**

Using the formula for osmotic pressure:

$\pi = CRT$

Where $\pi$ is the osmotic pressure, $C$ is the molarity, $R$ is the gas constant, and $T$ is the temperature.

Substituting the given values:

$4.92 = C \times 0.082 \times 300$

$4.92 = C \times 24.6$

$C = \dfrac{4.92}{24.6} = 0.2$ M

Answer: $0.2$ M

</details>

### Q55. MHT CET 2026 (13 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

2 g nonelectrolyte solute when dissolved in 50 g water increases its boiling point by 0.13 K.
Calculate the molar mass of solute if molal elevation constant of water is 0.52 K kg mol$^{-1}$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $140$ g/mol
- **(B)** $150$ g/mol
- **(C)** $160$ g/mol
- **(D)** $170$ g/mol

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $160$ g/mol**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

where $m$ is the molality of the solution, which can be expressed as:

$m = \dfrac{w_2 \times 1000}{M_2 \times w_1}$

Substituting the expression for molality into the boiling point elevation formula gives:

$\Delta T_b = \dfrac{K_b \times w_2 \times 1000}{M_2 \times w_1}$

Rearranging to solve for the molar mass of the solute, $M_2$:

$M_2 = \dfrac{K_b \times w_2 \times 1000}{\Delta T_b \times w_1}$

Substituting the given values ($K_b = 0.52$ K kg mol$^{-1}$, $w_2 = 2$ g, $\Delta T_b = 0.13$ K, $w_1 = 50$ g):

$M_2 = \dfrac{0.52 \times 2 \times 1000}{0.13 \times 50}$

$M_2 = 4 \times 40 = 160$ g/mol

</details>

### Q56. MHT CET 2026 (13 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Which of the following aqueous solutions will show maximum vapour pressure at 300K?

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$ M NaCl
- **(B)** $1$ M $\text{CaCl}_2$
- **(C)** $1$ M $\text{AlCl}_3$
- **(D)** $1$ M $\text{C}_{12}\text{H}_{22}\text{O}_{11}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $1$ M $\text{C}_{12}\text{H}_{22}\text{O}_{11}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Relative lowering of vapour pressure is a colligative property, which depends on the number of solute particles in the solution.

$\Delta P \propto i \times C$

For maximum vapour pressure, the lowering of vapour pressure ($\Delta P$) must be minimum, which means the van't Hoff factor ($i$) should be minimum (since concentration $C$ is $1$ M for all options).

For NaCl, $i = 2$
For $\text{CaCl}_2$, $i = 3$
For $\text{AlCl}_3$, $i = 4$
For $\text{C}_{12}\text{H}_{22}\text{O}_{11}$ (sucrose), $i = 1$

Since sucrose has the minimum value of $i$, its solution will have the minimum lowering of vapour pressure and hence the maximum vapour pressure.

Answer: $1$ M $\text{C}_{12}\text{H}_{22}\text{O}_{11}$

</details>

---

### Q57. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A solution of $50$g of solute 'X' is dissolved in $150$ g of 'Y' solvent boils at $357.27$ K. What is the molar mass of solute?
(Given $K_b$ and boiling point pure solvent is $2.77$ K kg/mol and $350.06$ K respectively.)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $132$ g/mol
- **(B)** $128$ g/mol
- **(C)** $150$ g/mol
- **(D)** $15$ g/mol

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $128$ g/mol**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by $\Delta T_b = T_b - T_b^0$.

$\Delta T_b = 357.27 - 350.06 = 7.21$ K.

The formula for elevation in boiling point is $\Delta T_b = K_b \times \dfrac{W_2 \times 1000}{M_2 \times W_1}$.

Substituting the given values:

$7.21 = 2.77 \times \dfrac{50 \times 1000}{M_2 \times 150}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{2.77 \times 50 \times 1000}{7.21 \times 150}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{2770}{21.63} \approx 128$ g/mol.

</details>

### Q58. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Identify from the following pair of solutions that exhibits the same osmotic pressure.
[molar mass of urea $= 60$ g mol$^{-1}$ and molar mass of glucose $= 180$ g mol$^{-1}$]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $6$ g/L urea solution and $18$ g/L glucose solution.
- **(B)** $12$ g/L urea solution and $18$ g/L glucose solution.
- **(C)** $18$ g/L urea solution and $18$ g/L glucose solution.
- **(D)** $6$ g/L urea solution and $6$ g/L glucose solution.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $6$ g/L urea solution and $18$ g/L glucose solution.**

**Detailed Solution:**

Osmotic pressure is given by $\pi = iCRT$.

For non-electrolytes like urea and glucose, the van't Hoff factor $i = 1$.

For two solutions to exhibit the same osmotic pressure at a given temperature, their molar concentrations must be equal ($C_1 = C_2$).

Molar concentration $C = \dfrac{\text{mass per unit volume}}{\text{molar mass}}$.

For $6$ g/L urea solution:
$C_{\text{urea}} = \dfrac{6}{60} = 0.1$ mol/L

For $18$ g/L glucose solution:
$C_{\text{glucose}} = \dfrac{18}{180} = 0.1$ mol/L

Since $C_{\text{urea}} = C_{\text{glucose}}$, these two solutions are isotonic and exhibit the same osmotic pressure.

Answer: $6$ g/L urea solution and $18$ g/L glucose solution.

</details>

### Q59. MHT CET 2025 (5 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate osmotic pressure of 0.1 M aqueous solution of an electrolyte at 300 K if van't Hoff factor is $1.125 .\left[\mathrm{R}=0.0821 \mathrm{~atm} \mathrm{dm}^3 \mathrm{~K}^{-1} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}\right]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 2.15 atm
- **(B)** 2.41 atm
- **(C)** 2.77 atm
- **(D)** 3.25 atm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 2.77 atm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The osmotic pressure of an electrolyte solution is given by the formula:

$\pi = iCRT$

Given:
$i = 1.125$
$C = 0.1 \text{ M} = 0.1 \text{ mol dm}^{-3}$
$R = 0.0821 \text{ atm dm}^3 \text{ K}^{-1} \text{ mol}^{-1}$
$T = 300 \text{ K}$

Substituting the given values into the formula:

$\pi = 1.125 \times 0.1 \times 0.0821 \times 300$

$\pi = 1.125 \times 2.463$

$\pi = 2.770875 \text{ atm}$

Rounding off to two decimal places, we get $2.77 \text{ atm}$.

Answer: 2.77 atm

</details>

### Q61. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which of the following aqueous solutions having same molality exhibits maximum boiling point elevation?
(Assume complete dissociation)

**Options:**

- **(A)** KCl
- **(B)** NaCl
- **(C)** $AlCl_3$
- **(D)** $BaCl_2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $AlCl_3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is a colligative property and is given by $\Delta T_b = i \times K_b \times m$.

For solutions with the same molality, $\Delta T_b \propto i$, where $i$ is the van't Hoff factor.

Assuming complete dissociation, the value of $i$ is equal to the number of ions produced per formula unit of the solute.

For KCl, $i = 2$ ($K^+$ and $Cl^-$).

For NaCl, $i = 2$ ($Na^+$ and $Cl^-$).

For $AlCl_3$, $i = 4$ ($Al^{3+}$ and $3Cl^-$).

For $BaCl_2$, $i = 3$ ($Ba^{2+}$ and $2Cl^-$).

Since $AlCl_3$ produces the maximum number of ions, it has the highest van't Hoff factor and exhibits the maximum boiling point elevation.

Answer: $AlCl_3$

</details>

### Q62. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate cryoscopic constant of solvent if depression in freezing point of 0.18 m solution of nonvolatile solute is 0.2 K

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.73 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $1.50 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $1.11 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $1.92 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1.11 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_f = K_f \times m$

Substituting the given values, $\Delta T_f = 0.2 \text{ K}$ and $m = 0.18 \text{ m}$:

$0.2 = K_f \times 0.18$

$K_f = \dfrac{0.2}{0.18}$

$K_f = \dfrac{20}{18} = 1.11 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$

Answer: $1.11 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$

</details>

### Q63. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the molality of the aqueous solution of nonvolatile solute if it freezes at $-0.36^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$. [ $\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{f}}$ for solvent $=1.86 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$ ]

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.218 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $0.193 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $0.401 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $0.520 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $0.193 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by:

$\Delta T_f = T_f^0 - T_f = 0^{\circ} \mathrm{C} - (-0.36^{\circ} \mathrm{C}) = 0.36 \mathrm{~K}$

Using the formula for depression in freezing point:

$\Delta T_f = K_f \times m$

$m = \dfrac{\Delta T_f}{K_f}$

Substituting the given values:

$m = \dfrac{0.36}{1.86}$

$m \approx 0.193 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$

Answer: $0.193 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$

</details>

### Q64. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

What is the numerical value of osmotic pressure of 1 M urea solution if numerical value of osmotic pressure of 0.5 M urea solution is ' $x$ '

**Options:**

- **(A)** $x$
- **(B)** $\frac{x}{2}$
- **(C)** $2 x$
- **(D)** $3 x$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $2 x$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Osmotic pressure is given by the formula $\pi = CRT$, where $C$ is the molar concentration, $R$ is the gas constant, and $T$ is the temperature.

For the $0.5$ M urea solution:
$\pi_1 = 0.5 RT = x$

For the $1$ M urea solution:
$\pi_2 = 1 RT$

Taking the ratio of the two osmotic pressures:
$\dfrac{\pi_2}{\pi_1} = \dfrac{1 RT}{0.5 RT} = 2$

$\pi_2 = 2 \pi_1 = 2x$

Answer: $2 x$

</details>

---

### Q65. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Arrange the following equimolar solutions according to increasing order of osmotic pressure [Assume complete ionisation]
A. KCl
B. $\mathrm{BaCl}_2$
C. $\mathrm{AlCl}_3$
D. $\mathrm{Al}_2\left(\mathrm{SO}_4\right)_3$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\mathrm{BaCl}_2 < \mathrm{Al}_2\left(\mathrm{SO}_4\right)_3 < \mathrm{KCl} < \mathrm{AlCl}_3$
- **(B)** $\mathrm{Al}_2\left(\mathrm{SO}_4\right)_3 < \mathrm{KCl} < \mathrm{BaCl}_2 < \mathrm{AlCl}_3$
- **(C)** $\mathrm{KCl} < \mathrm{BaCl}_2 < \mathrm{AlCl}_3 < \mathrm{Al}_2\left(\mathrm{SO}_4\right)_3$
- **(D)** $\mathrm{AlCl}_3 < \mathrm{BaCl}_2 < \mathrm{Al}_2\left(\mathrm{SO}_4\right)_3 < \mathrm{KCl}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\mathrm{KCl} < \mathrm{BaCl}_2 < \mathrm{AlCl}_3 < \mathrm{Al}_2\left(\mathrm{SO}_4\right)_3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Osmotic pressure is a colligative property, which depends on the number of solute particles in the solution.

For equimolar solutions, osmotic pressure $\pi \propto i$, where $i$ is the van 't Hoff factor.

Assuming complete ionization, the value of $i$ is equal to the number of ions produced per formula unit:
$\mathrm{KCl} \rightarrow \mathrm{K}^{+} + \mathrm{Cl}^{-}$ ($i = 2$)
$\mathrm{BaCl}_2 \rightarrow \mathrm{Ba}^{2+} + 2\mathrm{Cl}^{-}$ ($i = 3$)
$\mathrm{AlCl}_3 \rightarrow \mathrm{Al}^{3+} + 3\mathrm{Cl}^{-}$ ($i = 4$)
$\mathrm{Al}_2(\mathrm{SO}_4)_3 \rightarrow 2\mathrm{Al}^{3+} + 3\mathrm{SO}_4^{2-}$ ($i = 5$)

Since the value of $i$ increases in the order $\mathrm{KCl} < \mathrm{BaCl}_2 < \mathrm{AlCl}_3 < \mathrm{Al}_2(\mathrm{SO}_4)_3$, the osmotic pressure will also increase in the same order.

Answer: $\mathrm{KCl} < \mathrm{BaCl}_2 < \mathrm{AlCl}_3 < \mathrm{Al}_2\left(\mathrm{SO}_4\right)_3$

</details>

---

### Q66. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the number of moles of nonvolatile solute dissolved in 0.5 kg solvent if molal elevation constant for solvent is $2 \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$ $\left[\Delta \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{b}}=0.8 \mathrm{~K}\right]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 0.1
- **(B)** 0.2
- **(C)** 0.3
- **(D)** 0.4

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 0.2**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

where $m$ is the molality of the solution.

$0.8 = 2 \times m$

$m = \dfrac{0.8}{2} = 0.4 \text{ mol kg}^{-1}$

Molality is the number of moles of solute $n$ per kg of solvent $W$:

$m = \dfrac{n}{W}$

$0.4 = \dfrac{n}{0.5}$

$n = 0.4 \times 0.5 = 0.2 \text{ mol}$

Answer: 0.2

</details>

### Q67. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the vapour pressure of solution if relative lowering of vapour pressure and vapour pressure of pure solvent are 0.018 and 18 mm Hg respectively at 300 K .

**Options:**

- **(A)** 18.32 mm Hg
- **(B)** 17.08 mm Hg
- **(C)** 17.68 mm Hg
- **(D)** 18.60 mm Hg

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 17.68 mm Hg**

**Detailed Solution:**

The relative lowering of vapour pressure is given by the formula:

$\dfrac{P_1^0 - P_1}{P_1^0} = 0.018$

where $P_1^0$ is the vapour pressure of the pure solvent and $P_1$ is the vapour pressure of the solution.

Given $P_1^0 = 18$ mm Hg, substituting this value into the equation:

$\dfrac{18 - P_1}{18} = 0.018$

$18 - P_1 = 18 \times 0.018$

$18 - P_1 = 0.324$

$P_1 = 18 - 0.324 = 17.676$ mm Hg

Rounding to two decimal places, we get $17.68$ mm Hg.

Answer: 17.68 mm Hg

</details>

### Q68. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Calculate the molality of the solution containing nonvolatile solute if boiling point elevation of solution is 0.39 K .
$\left[\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{b}} \text { of water }=0.52 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}\right]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $0.52 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $0.65 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $0.75 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $0.86 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $0.75 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

where $\Delta T_b$ is the boiling point elevation, $K_b$ is the molal elevation constant, and $m$ is the molality of the solution.

Given:
$\Delta T_b = 0.39 \mathrm{~K}$
$K_b = 0.52 \mathrm{~K~kg~mol}^{-1}$

Substituting the values into the formula:

$0.39 = 0.52 \times m$

$m = \dfrac{0.39}{0.52}$

$m = \dfrac{39}{52} = \dfrac{3}{4} = 0.75 \mathrm{~mol~kg}^{-1}$

Answer: $0.75 \mathrm{~mol} \mathrm{~kg}^{-1}$

</details>

### Q69. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A solution of 5 g nonvolatile solute in 50 g water decreases its freezing point by 0.2 K . Calculate the molar mass of solute if $\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{f}}$ of water is $1.86 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $840 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$
- **(B)** $930 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$
- **(C)** $960 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$
- **(D)** $870 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $930 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given:
Mass of solute, $W_2 = 5 \text{ g}$
Mass of solvent, $W_1 = 50 \text{ g}$
Depression in freezing point, $\Delta T_f = 0.2 \text{ K}$
Cryoscopic constant, $K_f = 1.86 \text{ K kg mol}^{-1}$

The formula for depression in freezing point is:
$\Delta T_f = \dfrac{K_f \times W_2 \times 1000}{M_2 \times W_1}$

Substituting the given values:
$0.2 = \dfrac{1.86 \times 5 \times 1000}{M_2 \times 50}$

$0.2 = \dfrac{1.86 \times 100}{M_2}$

$0.2 = \dfrac{186}{M_2}$

$M_2 = \dfrac{186}{0.2} = 930 \text{ g mol}^{-1}$

Answer: $930 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$

</details>

### Q70. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Which from following pairs of solutions in water exhibits same osmotic pressure at same temperature ?
$\left[\right.$ molar mass of urea $=60 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$, sucrose $\left.=342 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}\right]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ urea and $17.1 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ sucrose
- **(B)** $6 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ urea and $17.1 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ sucrose
- **(C)** $3 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ urea and $34.2 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ sucrose
- **(D)** $6 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ urea and $8.6 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ sucrose

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $3 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ urea and $17.1 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ sucrose**

**Detailed Solution:**

Osmotic pressure is given by $\pi = C R T$, where $C$ is the molar concentration.

For solutions of non-electrolytes to have the same osmotic pressure at the same temperature, their molar concentrations must be equal.

Molar concentration $C = \dfrac{\text{Mass per litre}}{\text{Molar mass}}$.

For $3 \text{ g L}^{-1}$ urea, $C = \dfrac{3}{60} = 0.05 \text{ mol L}^{-1}$.

For $17.1 \text{ g L}^{-1}$ sucrose, $C = \dfrac{17.1}{342} = 0.05 \text{ mol L}^{-1}$.

Since both solutions have the same molar concentration, they exhibit the same osmotic pressure.

Answer: $3 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ urea and $17.1 \mathrm{~g} \mathrm{~L}^{-1}$ sucrose

</details>

---

### Q71. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 2)

Calculate the osmotic pressure of 0.2 M aqueous solution of electrolyte at 300 K . If van't Hoff factor is 1.6
$\left[\mathrm{R}=0.0821 \mathrm{~atm} \mathrm{dm}^3 \mathrm{~K}^{-1} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}\right]$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 7.21 atm
- **(B)** 7.88 atm
- **(C)** 8.81 atm
- **(D)** 8.32 atm

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 7.88 atm**

**Detailed Solution:**

The formula for osmotic pressure is $\pi = iCRT$.

Given:
$i = 1.6$
$C = 0.2$ M
$R = 0.0821$ atm dm$^3$ K$^{-1}$ mol$^{-1}$
$T = 300$ K

Substituting the values:
$\pi = 1.6 \times 0.2 \times 0.0821 \times 300$

$\pi = 0.32 \times 24.63$

$\pi = 7.8816$ atm

Rounding off, we get $7.88$ atm.

Answer: 7.88 atm

### Q72. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 2)

- **(B)** $4.5 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$

### Q74. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Calculate van't Hoff factor of aqueous solution of 0.18 m electrolyte that freezes at $-0.54^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$
$\left(\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{f}} \text { for solvent }=1.86 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}\right)$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1.126$
- **(B)** $2.449$
- **(C)** $1.612$
- **(D)** $2.150$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $1.612$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_f = i \times K_f \times m$

We have:
$\Delta T_f = 0.54^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$
$K_f = 1.86 \mathrm{~K~kg~mol^{-1}}$
$m = 0.18 \mathrm{~m}$

Substituting the values into the formula:

$0.54 = i \times 1.86 \times 0.18$

$i = \dfrac{0.54}{1.86 \times 0.18}$

$i = \dfrac{0.54}{0.3348}$

$i \approx 1.612$

Answer: $1.612$

</details>

### Q75. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Determine the expected value of $\Delta \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{f}}$ for $1 \mathrm{~m}$ $\mathrm{CaCl}_2$ solution if 1 m urea solution has $\Delta \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{f}}$ value ' $x$ ' K .

**Options:**

- **(A)** $x \mathrm{~K}$
- **(B)** $2x \mathrm{~K}$
- **(C)** $3x \mathrm{~K}$
- **(D)** $\frac{x}{2} \mathrm{~K}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $3x \mathrm{~K}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The depression in freezing point is given by $\Delta T_f = i K_f m$.

For urea, it is a non-electrolyte, so the van 't Hoff factor $i = 1$.

$\Delta T_f (\text{urea}) = 1 \times K_f \times 1 = x \Rightarrow K_f = x$

For $\text{CaCl}_2$, it is a strong electrolyte and dissociates into one $\text{Ca}^{2+}$ ion and two $\text{Cl}^-$ ions, so $i = 3$.

$\Delta T_f (\text{CaCl}_2) = 3 \times K_f \times 1 = 3x \text{ K}$

Answer: $3x \mathrm{~K}$

</details>

### Q76. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

When 0.01 mole of nonvolatile solute is dissolved in certain solvent calculate the mass of solvent in kg if $\Delta \mathrm{T}_{\mathrm{b}}=0.6 \mathrm{~K}$ and $\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{b}}$ for solvent $=2 \mathrm{~K} \mathrm{~kg} \mathrm{~mol}^{-1}$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 0.014 kg
- **(B)** 0.028 kg
- **(C)** 0.033 kg
- **(D)** 0.045 kg

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 0.033 kg**

**Detailed Solution:**

The elevation in boiling point is given by the formula:

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times m$

where $m$ is the molality of the solution, which is defined as the number of moles of solute divided by the mass of the solvent in kg ($W$).

$\Delta T_b = K_b \times \dfrac{n}{W}$

Rearranging the formula to solve for the mass of the solvent $W$:

$W = \dfrac{K_b \times n}{\Delta T_b}$

Substituting the given values ($n = 0.01$ mol, $\Delta T_b = 0.6$ K, $K_b = 2$ K kg mol$^{-1}$):

$W = \dfrac{2 \times 0.01}{0.6}$

$W = \dfrac{0.02}{0.6} = \dfrac{1}{30} \text{ kg}$

$W \approx 0.033 \text{ kg}$

Answer: 0.033 kg

</details>
